#6982. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

#6980. Mark Rober's Class CrunchLabs Courses Now Available for Free on YouTube

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. #6976. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#6974. How to Style Flannel Shirts Like the Cool Girls

Muted plaids, sleek layers, and smart contrasts make flannel feel instantly elevated.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Once associated solely with the lumberjack aesthetic, flannel shirts have become a wardrobe staple. Flannel is, at its core, a remarkably versatile fabric—soft, structured, and warm without bulk, with a texture that looks expensive when styled with intention. It’s timeless, perfect for in-between weather, and, you have to admit, effortlessly cool.

The trick to elevating it lies in contrast. Pair a brushed-cotton shirt with sharp tailoring and it instantly looks considered. These days, flannel is being worn with everything from denim to satin skirts, and each pairing adds up to an elevated outfit formula.

Beyond the pairings, the details do the heavy lifting. Choose muted, earthy plaids over loud ones, and let the shirt’s softness play against something sleek, like pleated trousers or an oversized fit thrown over a slip dress. Layer it under a cozy coat or knot it at the waist, and flannel stops feeling like a seasonal afterthought. It becomes the piece the whole outfit is built around.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up elevated flannel outfit ideas to inspire you, built around fall’s most essential piece.

The unexpected pairing of black cargo shorts, a blue check flannel, and black loafers works surprisingly well. It reads as casual with a side of cool-girl attitude.

Pairing a practical flannel shirt with a romantic pink satin skirt and black flip-flops brings a fresh approach to fall styling. Trade the flip-flops for ankle boots and add a cropped jacket when temperatures drop.

We love how this outfit pairs an everyday plaid top with wrap-skirt trousers and statement yellow sneakers. The pop of color keeps the look playful, while the easy plaid grounds it in classic fall style.

Styled with wide-leg jeans, this look reads casual, but it’s the luxe accessories that instantly elevate it. A quilted Chanel bag, sleek loafers, and oversized sunglasses add polish without losing the easy feel of the denim.

Tied around the waist, this flannel shirt pairs seamlessly with a checked skirt, even with their different prints. Worn with a chunky knit and shearling suede jacket, every layer and detail feels intentional.

#6972. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#6971. Millennial Designers Are Thriving, That’s Also Good News For Gen Z

Style is getting more personal; here’s who to thank.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As someone who sits squarely in the millennial category (1990 was a good year), I find it inspiring to see more and more of my peers taking the helm of fashion’s most important labels. Think Grace Wales Bonner (also 1990) at Hermès, Matthieu Blazy (1984) at Chanel, and Maximilian Davis (1995) at Ferragamo. Add to that the culturally important work of indie darlings like Christopher John Rogers and Sandy Liang, and I feel like fashion is speaking my language, or at least sharing my references.

But these compatriots of mine (victims of millennial cringe, purpose-driven overworkers) are also the perfect people to connect with Gen Z. They understand online culture and trends in a way that those before them never could, while still having the analog knowledge to bring fresh ideas to the table.

Yes, millennials as a whole may never shake the “blanding” allegations that came from the rise of quiet luxury and minimalist basics, but the generation’s most prolific designers are more interested in having fun with the clothes they’re creating. They can withstand (or even encourage) controversy (hello, Chanel’s soleless sandals) while still offering pieces we actually want to wear. And for Gen Z, a generation defined by the pursuit of personal style, that sense of play is the key to unlocking how they want to dress.

The rise of The RealReal has become a signifier of the shifting attitudes around personal style. What began as a platform focused on access to pre-loved luxury items for lower prices has become a destination for one-of-a-kind finds that make a wardrobe feel personal. Gen Z’s obsession with self-expression is well-suited to one-of-a-kind vintage and modern designers who have carved out a more playful, personal point of view.

Beyond secondhand, here are some of the millennial designers I respect who are also naturally tuned in to younger generations and who I think will define fashion for years to come.

Liang’s girlishly whimsical designs feel like they’ve been plucked from my childhood bedroom, while also leaning into the aesthetics of creatives like Sofia Coppola and Petra Collins.

Hillary Taymour (1987) has zeroed in on sustainable, upcycled clothes, often modeled by her diverse, wildly creative group of industry friends. They’re clothes that straddle the line between Nirvana-tinged grunge and fairycore.

Fresh off a new J. Crew collaboration, Eckhaus Latta is a New York brand bringing a sense of cool to everyday staples, from tie-dye jeans to quirky knits. Consider it the place to build an everyday wardrobe that feels different.

Now overseeing menswear at Hermès, Grace Wales Bonner also has her own label and a widely beloved Adidas collaboration under her belt. Her creations tend to be sporty yet classic, weaving in ’70s flair and unusual details like grommets that make each piece feel especially cool.

#6970. The 14 Best Leather Colognes That Are Perfect for the Cold Weather Ahead

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Just as versatile as your favorite leather jacket.

You pass a shoe store, a real one with old-school styles, and catch that scent of new leather. You slip on an old beat up leather jacket and get that deep, squeaky earthy hit. You slide into a leather banquette at your town’s most popular restaurant. Ever wanted that scent, in any variety, bottled up? That’s what a leather fragrance gives you. Warm, rich, and earthy, and often with a smoky edge, they’re especially great for fall and winter, though the right one can work year-round.

Even vegetarians like myself can get behind a sexy leather cologne; they’re not distilling real animal leather, if you were wondering. Since leather has no extractable essential oil that can be used for perfume, perfumers recreate its scent using synthetic or botanical accords, often blended with smoky, resinous notes like birch tar, cade, and labdanum. It’s creating the idea of leather. The result can range from rugged and intense to soft and smooth like suede. There are plenty of different takes on leather fragrance out there, but these are our favorites.

This fragrance from Cremo mostly features white moss and amber, but the base notes of smooth suede come through at the end to create a warm, elevated aroma. Don’t be fooled by the low price point—you’ll still get a sophisticated scent of suede, though it’s less aggressive than its spicier and woodier alternatives.

This Vintage cologne from John Varvatos is about as manly as it gets. Its warm, woody overall scent with hints of patchouli and tobacco leaf underneath leave you with a long-lasting air of sophistication and refinement. The warm spice notes perfectly complement crisp autumn air while the patchouli and suede offer warmth during the winter like a fireplace in a cozy cabin.

There’s no mistaking the leather in this cologne—it permeates the entire package—but instead of being aggressive, it comes across as sophisticated. It’s a gentlemanly scent that’s surprisingly wearable for both day and night, thanks to the amber and patchouli at the base that give it a warm, herbal vibe. Think of it as less “cowboy” and more “oil baron.”

If Ombré Leather is Tom Ford’s easy, toned-down version of leather, Tuscan Leather is the aggressive, literal version. The leather almost overpowers everything else—almost—but the sweeter top notes allow for a delicious opening that adds smoothness to the leather and the warm, woody dry down lends a layered elegance. The leather here is top quality. Not new car smell, but perfectly worn-in bespoke shoes or the seats of a vintage Ferrari.

At first spray, the fruity plum and peach top notes take over, but pretty soon they give way to a deeper, more layered fragrance that really does call to mind the stacks of an old library. The sweetness doesn’t entirely go away, but as the florals and leather appear, like pages turning in a book, more depth is revealed.

Designed as an enhancer—a lighter fragrance to layer with others to change the vibe—this interpretation of leather smells almost exactly like the real thing. It’s slick, smooth, and warm, with a sharp edge that’s masculine in a ’70s Camaro kind of way. Sure, you can wear it as it was meant (that is, with another cologne), but we say try it by itself to really drive that leather home.

Tru Western’s Leather Vintage Label cologne collection is a great place to start when it comes to leather fragrances. Each of the five scents highlights a different aspect of leather, but No. 1 brings out woody notes—and a bit of fresh clementine—to give the leather accord a classic, straightforward backdrop that lets the leather accord be the star player. For something a bit richer with amber notes, try No. 4 (Deep Leather).

We’re big fans of Ranger Station, the Nashville-based niche fragrance brand known for its nature-inspired scents—and it all started with the Leather + Pine candle. After the candle took off, Ranger Station of course had to turn the scent into a wearable fragrance. Slightly different from the candle’s scent, the EDP is a blend of tanned leather, pine sap, amber, and sandalwood, which creates an alluring blend of sweet and savory.

We can’t talk about leather colognes without mentioning Malin + Goetz’s famous take on the scent. Simply called “leather,” it’s a rugged blend of florals, spices, and woods that emphasizes pepper and lily of the valley along with leather accord. And while it features a handful of floral notes, it never leans into powdery territory; it remains a solidly masculine scent, though it suits pretty much everyone.

For a bit of a more intense, spicy take on leather, Desert Nights is the cologne to go with. It’s inspired by Marrakech after sunset, layering luxurious oud and sandalwood with earthy patchouli and moss, and the spices up top carry through. It’s seductive and perfect for a date night or winter outing.

Arquiste is known for complex fragrances inspired by historical events and specific landscapes, and Nocturnality is one of our favorites—we even included it in our 2025 Cologne Awards. The inspiration for this one comes from mid-’90s New York City nightlife; picture an underground club with humid air, booze, cigarette smoke, and lots of people dancing. In a less abstract sense, that means latex, leather, and oud, which combine for a dark, sexy scent.

Most leather colognes lean on warmth, sweetness, and spice to complement the leather notes (which is partly why you often see a shift toward leather colognes in the winter), but this one does things a bit differently. Yes, there’s spice in the opening, but there’s also citrus that moves on to florals in the middle before drying down to earthy, musky notes. The leather is apparent all the way through, but it feels lighter and fresher than many others on this list.

There’s something very nostalgic about this fragrance but it doesn’t smell old-fashioned. Instead, it calls to mind dark rooms, sexy rendezvous, sweaty nights, and skin tight jeans. It’s smoky and spicy, but layered with musk and pungent black leather for an über masculine vibe that’s impossible to ignore. When you wear it, expect to be noticed—and for the compliments to come nonstop.

Perfumehead’s scents, like all the best fragrances, tell stories and paint pictures. This boozy, smoky juice is inspired by the cowboy-filled honky-tonks of the 1970s Sunset Strip, and one whiff is more than enough to take you there. The final effect of the fragrance isn’t especially Western or aggressive, but more cozy and sensual, like finding a vintage leather jacket that miraculously fits you like a glove.

#6967. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

#6966. The 4 Common Pitfalls of Long Distance Relationships, According to Experts

The good news is you can totally overcome them all.

You’re convinced you’ve met the love of your life. They’re charming, they’re hot, and god, they’re funny. When you’re together, it’s butterflies and kismet, but there’s one thing wrong: you live in different cities. Or worse, in different states altogether.

Long-distance adds an undoubtedly difficult component to a relationship, but not an impossible one. Many long-term and married couples have dealt with being long-distance at one point or another, and have lived to tell the tale. So, what’s the secret? A little extra dose of savvy with a touch more navigation.

I don’t want to make it sound like it’s easy-peasy though—LDRs aren’t for the weak and can be an uphill battle. So to get the full rundown on what makes long-distance relationships successful—and fails—we asked experts about their most common pitfalls. From mismatched communication styles to the sheer lack of physical touch and intimacy, below are some of the four most common reasons long-distance relationships fail.

For some couples, this feels like an impossible hurdle to overcome. “Physical affection, flirting, feeling desired, and just being close to someone are all important parts of a relationship,” says Carly S., pleasure educator and owner of Horny Stoner. “When you don't have easy access to those things, you have to get a little more creative and actually make an effort to keep that connection going,”

Perhaps just as crucial and challenging is that long-distance couples don’t get to witness the mundane moments in each others’ lives. “When you're not physically together, you miss out on many of the smaller, everyday interactions that help people feel connected,” says Marina Gershkovich, PhD, a New-York based cognitive behavioral therapist specializing in anxiety and relationships. “It's the spontaneous conversations, shared routines, and little moments that happen when you're spending time together,” she continues, referring to the common things that are experienced in non-long-distance relationships.

“You don't have those little everyday interactions that happen naturally when you're around someone. Instead, you're relying on texts, phone calls, and trying to make time for each other, which can leave a lot of room for misunderstandings,” Carly S. adds.

Despite these inevitable hurdles, with a little creativity, maintaining physical and emotional intimacy with your partner can be done. “A good supplement for not seeing each other in person is scheduling date nights,” Carley S. suggests. “That can be spending time watching a movie together on video call, a longer weekly phone call, sexting, or sex toys, especially ones that can be controlled long-distance, so it's more interactive.”

Angelika Koch, relationships & breakup expert at Taimi, adds on: “You can also send them small gifts like a stuffed animal to cuddle with, a weighted blanket to ease anxiety, and there are vibrating bracelets that allow with a simple tap, you to have a moment of distant touch.”

I’ve seen this with many of my friends in long-distance relationships. The anticipation to see someone you only get to lay eyes on a few times a year is exciting, it’s almost like a perpetual honeymoon effect. However, when it comes time to actually spend real time together, sometimes the facade fades away. Turns out, there’s a name for that: idealization, or when you like the idea of someone or something much more than the person or thing itself.

“Idealization refers to what happens to our perceptions during long periods of separation,” says relationship expert Wendy Walsh, PhD, a psychology professor at California State University Channel Islands. “Some people create an imaginary ‘perfect boyfriend or girlfriend’ in their minds that doesn’t match up with the real-life version who finally arrives for a visit.”

This isn’t exclusive to long-distance relationships, but since communication is such a key element for long-distance relationships, having different communication styles and preferences can undoubtedly create a bigger hurdle. And it’s not just the way we communicate, but having different expectations for how often communication should happen.

“One person might want to talk several times a day, while the other is perfectly comfortable checking in once,” says Gershkovich. “Neither approach is necessarily wrong, but if those expectations aren't communicated or discussed, it's easy for one or both partners to feel frustrated or neglected.”

She continues: “Distance can also leave more room for assumptions. If someone doesn't respond to a text or misses a phone call, it's easier to start filling in the blanks, particularly for someone who might struggle with anxiety or insecurity.”

That spiral sometimes can lead to jealousy issues, though of course, those aren’t exclusive to LDRs. “Trust and jealousy issues aren't necessarily more common in long-distance relationships, but distance can amplify insecurities that are already there. The distance might act as a magnifying glass.” She continues, “When we don't have all the information, our minds tend to fill in the gaps. A delayed response to a text can suddenly feel like evidence that something is wrong, even when there's no real reason to think that.”

Keep the golden rule of “quality over quantity” in mind when it comes to communicating with your long-distance partner, and don’t assume more of something is the quick-fix.

“More communication isn't necessarily better communication,” Gershkovich says. “Sometimes texting all day can start to feel like an obligation, and it doesn't always make people feel any closer. Couples can benefit from focusing on the quality of their interactions and that quality will be determined by their own preferences.”

At the end of the day, the biggest issue in a long-distance relationship is unaligned future values together (and similar to the above, this isn’t only applicable to LDRs).

“One of the biggest reasons long-distance relationships might fail is that couples might start to lose their sense of connection or stop feeling like they're working toward the same ‘future,’” Gershkovich explains. “Sometimes relationships also end because people's needs or circumstances change that might change their version of what they might want their ‘future’ to look like.”

Does this mean you need to discuss your future early on in the relationship? Perhaps, and it’s really a couple-by-couple basis to make that determination. However, sharing similar goals for the future can strengthen any relationship, not just a long-distance one.

“Having a shared vision for the future can also make a big difference, particularly when both people feel they're working toward eventually being in the same place,” Gershkovich continues. “Long-distance relationships can be just as healthy and fulfilling as relationships where couples live close to each other. They do, however, require more deliberate effort to maintain connection, communicate needs, and make time for each other.”

#6964. How to Pull Off Coffee Nails, From Creamy Latte Shades to Deep Espresso

The cozy brown manicure trend works as a glossy neutral, a velvet cat-eye, or a caramel French tip.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a lot to love about coffee, and even if you’re not a fan of the drink, the color itself, with a rich, deep brown, serves as the perfect neutral to pair with your favorite light or dark shade. On nails, it works seamlessly across skin tones. And don’t think that coffee brown is only reserved for cooler winter months. Nail experts share how to wear the shade as a base, accent, or main character color this fall and beyond.

Max Estrada, a professional nail artist and founder and creative director of eNailCouture based in Tucson, Arizona, calls the coffee shade “latte lacquer” to describe its range in the rich brown color family. “It’s essentially the beauty equivalent of walking into a gorgeous Italian café and being presented with an entire menu of indulgent coffee creations,” he says. “Think creamy cappuccino, warm caramel, rich mocha, toasted hazelnut, and deep espresso.” It’s a beautiful spectrum of warm, sophisticated neutrals inspired by different shades and textures of coffee.

Estrada notes that the trend ties into the gourmand fragrance movement. “We’ve fallen in love with perfumes that smell like vanilla, caramel, chocolate, and coffee, and now we’re bringing that same delicious, comforting luxury to our fingertips,” Estrada shares. According to Estrada, what separates coffee from other brown polish shades is its depth and richness.

When choosing your version of a coffee manicure, Viktoriia Voloshyna, international judge, award-winning nail educator, and founder of Tory Pro Nails Studio in Beverly Hills, suggests thinking of a coffee menu, from light to dark:Milk foam: a soft ivory with a gentle glowFlat white: a warm, creamy beigeLatte: a soft satin beigeCaramel: a rich, warm brownEspresso: a deep coffee brown or a dark chocolate brown

“All these shades mix beautifully on one hand. You can go from light to dark, like a gradient from milk to espresso, or do milky nails with one espresso accent nail,” says Voloshyna.

If very light shades aren’t your norm, Estrada recommends starting with a warm caramel latte, meaning creamy beige, toasted almond, or buttery caramel shades. For something more dramatic, he suggests rich mocha, dark chocolate, and espresso tones, particularly those with warm undertones that complement the natural complexion of the hands. “Personally, I think the secret is choosing colors with beautiful depth rather than flat, lifeless browns,” says Estrada.

To create the depth and dimension Estrada and Voloshyna refer to, mix a few coffee shades into a French manicure, a blended ombré, or a magnetic cat-eye finish. “One technique I particularly love is creating a beautiful caramel or chocolate French manicure and then layering a sheer milky nude over the entire design,” says Estrada. “It softens the contrast and creates this gorgeous creamy, diffused effect, almost like watching milk swirl into a freshly poured espresso.”

Although the darkness of coffee lacquer feels more seasonally appropriate in fall and winter, Voloshyna says it’s easily adaptable in spring and summer, too. “In spring and summer, I would go lighter and more sheer, like an iced latte: milky, translucent shades with a glossy finish. In fall and winter, it’s all about deeper mocha and espresso, and you can add cat-eye or a little shimmer for the holidays,” says Voloshyna.

For the lighter looks, Estrada also recommends a delicate caramel French tip or blended ombré finishes that combine the lighter latte shades.

In winter, Estrada goes more extravagant with deep mocha shades accented with gold chrome, espresso with a velvet magnetic finish, or rich chocolate with delicate metallic embellishments. “It’s the same beautiful color family, but with an entirely different personality depending on the season,” he adds.

The range of a coffee manicure works on different nail shapes, too. “A beautifully tapered, elongated almond nail in a rich mocha shade is incredibly sophisticated and almost sensual. It reminds me of the elegant, exaggerated fashion silhouettes of the 1990s. For something more architectural, I love a dramatic, boxy square nail with a caramel French manicure. It’s wonderfully nostalgic and fashionable,” explains Estrada.

Shorter nails can certainly wear the trend too, especially in a single warm cappuccino shade—but Estrada says longer extensions leave room to experiment with more elaborate gradients, sculpted details, and multidimensional finishes.

If you do go dark on longer nails, though, Voloshyna says to keep the nails thin, especially if you’re painting over a gel or acrylic base. “Coffee shades, especially the dark ones, make thick nails look heavy,” she notes.

The finish you choose changes everything, so choose wisely. “A beautiful high-gloss caramel manicure resembles liquid toffee or freshly poured espresso. It's rich, polished, and incredibly luxurious. But a matte finish creates an entirely different personality. Deep chocolate and mocha shades suddenly resemble suede, velvet, or the finest cashmere,” describes Estrada. Feel free to combine textures, too. Try a matte espresso nail with an ultra-glossy French tip or a chocolate magnetic cat-eye to create the look of crushed velvet.

Another technique Estrada uses to create dimension is layering a sheer milky color over glitter. Begin with a champagne, gold, or caramel glitter, then apply a translucent milky nude over the top. “Instead of having that very sharp, obvious sparkle, the glitter becomes beautifully diffused beneath the creamy layer,” he says. Think precious jewelry viewed through frosted glass. “The sparkle is still there, but it has this gorgeous soft-focus quality. It doesn’t actually make the manicure matte, but it softens the visual intensity of the glitter, and you can choose a glossy or matte topcoat depending on the final look you want.”

With coffee, Voloshyna prefers a glossy finish to keep the color rich and creamy, like fresh coffee. Matte topcoats are reserved for mocha and espresso manis to give a soft, velvety look. “In winter, a brown cat-eye adds depth and makes the color look like melted chocolate,” she concludes.

#6962. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

#6955. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#6953. Two-Tone Heels Are the Playful Shoe Trend Your Fall Wardrobe Needs

Add a bold, quirky finish to your monochrome outfits.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Whether your wardrobe is ready or not, one thing is clear: designers want us to wear more color. The message came through on several spring/summer 2027 runways, including Carolina Herrera, LII, and Miu Miu. Footwear is where the idea gets especially playful.

Two-tone shoes have been trending all year, largely thanks to Matthieu Blazy’s work at Chanel, where bicolor heels on the fall 2026 runway showed that fashion doesn’t have to take itself so seriously. A little quirkiness is the whole point. Bold yet unexpected, the two-tone shoe is a fun surprise against an otherwise tonal outfit.

The best part is how easy it is to try. You don’t need to rebuild your closet or commit to a head-to-toe color story. Start at your feet by pairing a split-color heel with a monochrome look, and let the shoes be the only loud note. If you want to go further, pick one shade from the shoe and echo it in a bag, a scarf, or a lip color. The contrast does the work, and the outfit looks intentional rather than overthought.

#6950. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6949. It’s Time to Buy an Art TV. These Are the 4 Best Options.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are slim, glare-free, and give your house an extra touch of class.

Whether you call it an art TV, a frame TV, or a gallery TV, we’re all talking about the same thing. An anti-reflective matte screen, paired with a thin (often wood or wood-looking) bezel so that when it’s turned off you can approximate a gallery painting or photographs instead of staring at a blank black screen. These trendy TVs are the new best way for home design lovers to not compromise their aesthetic visions while still entertaining. Art TVs are for the people who care about their home design as much as their actual TV watching experience.

The breakout, genre-defining example was The Frame. Samsung’s 2017 release was the first TV model that truly masqueraded as an art piece on your wall. Instead of a big blank space on your wall, you now have art when you're not watching TV, and a TV when you are. Got a date coming over? Switch that black screen to one of any classic artworks from The Met or one of many museums worldwide. It took a few iterations to get right, but less than a decade later Samsung was doing enough right that other TV manufacturers, Hisense, LG, and TCL are following suit.

These four art TVs are a marvelous blend of form and function. I tried to pick a range of sizes between 50 and 75 inches, but most of these TVs come in every size between 55 inches and 85 inches. Whatever your budget or space allows, you’ll find the right one here.

Samsung still makes the best art TV out there. With the introduction of the Frame Pro line, Samsung made the first premium art TV. Premium picture quality, premium sound (for once), and—if you’re willing to pay a handsome $4.99 a month—the largest selection of fine art you can get on one of these. Even for the low price, it’s annoying to essential pay the Met a subscription fee to use one of the main features of your TV. But, here’s the upside. It’s all real art and no AI; refreshing in this economy. The free library cycles through about 30 new pieces of art a month, so even if you aren’t paying you’re getting a new influx of (real) art periodically.

Free with your package is Samsung's One Connect Box. This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics. It opens you up to put your Frame anywhere the TV fits on your wall, while your cable box and gaming consoles are plugged into the OCB and stowed out of sight. The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.

Compared to the more ubiquitous Google TV experience you’ll get on the Hisense and TCL, Samsung’s user interface takes some getting used to if you haven’t driven one of these TVs before. Even with familiarity, it’s still not my favorite to navigate.

The Frame Pro might be the flagship, but Samsung is still updating its regular Frame model each year with great success. This 2026 update to the original Frame is a slightly more affordable, slightly less impressive version of what you get with the Frame Pro. It’s equipped with everything Samsung has figured out over the years: an anti-reflective matte display, options for customizable bezels, and a massive accumulated gallery of artwork to choose from.

It may not technically reach the heights of the Pro, but the picture quality is super clear and the colors are vivd enough for most viewers to be satisfied. On the whole, it has a lot of the same pros (and all the same cons) as the beefier Pro for a few hundred dollars cheaper.

If you need something smaller than 55-inches, this is the only art TV that clears the bar. Luckily, it is a very good one. The 2026 Hisense Canvas looks and sounds fantastic, the setup is way more streamlined than The Frame, and it runs Google TV, which has become my OS of choice due to the consistent performance and easy setup. In my team’s tests, we found this to be the best looking art TV for the money, even if it technically fell short of the Frame Pro.

The best part of the Hisense art TV experience is that you don't have to pay for the art. Not once, not ever. Plus, the CanvasTV even offers a 144 Hz refresh rate mode for gaming with low latency. Not that I’d recommend gaming on an art TV, but if it’s your only option, Hisense makes it at least tolerable. We only wish we had the convenience of the Samsung OCB with the free art offerings of the CanvasTV. Now, that would be heaven.

Dollars to donuts, this 75-inch TCL art TV is the best value in the category, costing roughly as much as a 55-inch Samsung Frame Pro. The budget-friendly display comes at the cost of performance and color. It's got muddier blacks that can make night scenes hard to watch and it's not as vibrant as The Frame or the CanvasTV.

For one, this TV does have one advantage that helps it outweigh the others; it's the only frame TV that comes close to being as thin as an actual piece of artwork. It only has one bezel option, but at least it’s a relatively neutral off-white.

The art selection is decent, and we like how easy the TV and Google OS makes it to share your own photos instead. TCL also boasts about the hundreds of thousands of pieces of AI artwork you can access and create. It solves the lack of content problem, but a computer plagiarizing Van Gogh is never going to be a fraction as beautiful as a picture of an actual Van Gogh.

In short, art TVs are meant to be able to double as framed artwork when they are not in use. The features of these TVs include:

For me, it’s got to be 65-inches. Even in my (relatively) small Brooklyn apartment, this is the sweet spot for TV size. Anything larger is really just for shits and giggles at that point. 55 inches is generally the smallest (aside from Hisense with its 50 inch) size of art TV you can get and it’s largely pretty acceptable, but know if you have a large wall that even that may feel small.

The OS is basically the whole look and feel of a smart TV interface. Roku City is comforting, but you won't get that here. The only options are Google TV or Samusng's Tizen OS. Between the Tizen interface and Google TV, we give the slight edge to the Google UI and OS. It's smoother and gives more options for integrating Google Photos—a great feature to have on an art TV. As far as TV operating systems go, though, they are pretty similar in terms of layout and amount of space dedicated to advertising.

We've done the hard work for you by spending years watching these TVs up close. We've seen them in action on showroom floors, in private demo sessions, and (most important of all) in our homes. We've awarded the best of the best. Needless to say, we did all the heavy lifting for you. Sometimes literally.

#6947. Meet the ‘Dexter: Resurrection’ Season 2 Cast & Characters

Just in time for Halloween, one of television’s bloodiest (in sooo many ways) leading men is back. Ready to dive into Dexter: Resurrection season 2? Here’s everything you need to know about the Dexter sequel series cast and characters.

Fans of Dexter who hated the antihero’s fate in the original series finale probably feel so vindicated right now. Has there ever been a bigger “never mind” in pop culture? I don’t know! You honestly might as well call this show Dexter: Retcon. It is a sequel to Dexter: New Blood, which was also of course a sequel to Dexter. The first season took the “good” serial killer to New York City in search of his grown-up son. But fatherly duties can’t keep him from pulling out the plastic sheets. A new city actually leds to so, so many opportunities for dark vengeance. In that first season, Dexter met a “club” of serial killers lead by a character played by Peter Dinklage. What next? Season 2 promises an equally impressive murderers’ row.

Spoiler alert, but you’ll still see David Zayas play Angel Batista, even though he died at the end of Season 1, in some capacity this season. You’ll also continue to see James Remar play Harry Morgan, Dexter’s deceased father and spiritual guide. As for the other main characters, new and returning, here’s a who’s who.

Dexter is, as I’m sure you know, a serial killer whose adopted father Harry “trained” to have a strict moral code and only murder other killers and dangerous criminals.

Michael C. Hall has now played his best-known character on Dexter, Dexter: New Blood, Dexter: Original Sin, and Dexter: Resurrection. Before that, he was already pretty well known for playing David Fisher on HBO’s Six Feet Under. He played John F. Kennedy in one episode of The Crown. He is a Golden Globe and SAG Award winner. Hall is also a winning stage actor whose Broadway credits alone include Chicago, Cabaret, and Hedwig and the Angry Inch. Theatre-lovers also know that he starred in the David Bowie musical Lazarus off-Broadway and in London. And some of his film credits include Game Night, Kill Your Darlings, and Christine.

Harrison is Dexter and Rita’s now grown-up son. This season he’s joining the NYPD.

Jack Alcott first started playing Harrison in Dexter: New Blood back in 2021. He was also a lead on the miniseries The Good Lord Bird. He guest starred in Poker Face and The Blacklist. To this date, he only has one film credit: the 2025 supernatural western Killing Faith.

Charley is a security guard who worked for a rich but late serial killer.

Over 20 years later, Uma Thurman is still probably best known for the Kill Bill duology and Pulp Fiction. Let’s never, ever forget that she played Poison Ivy in Batman & Robin, either. She is an Academy Award-nominated and Golden Globe-winning actress. While she’s not really known for television, some of her previous small screen credits include Suspicion, Hysterical Blindness, Chambers, NBC’s The Slap, and NBC’s Smash. It’s very possible that the last thing you saw her in was Red, White & Royal Blue as the President of the United States... NBD!

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine plays Blessing Kamara

Blessing is a rideshare driver, Dexter’s landlord, and first friend in NYC.

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine played Ronnie Davis on The Chi for 30 episodes and Jacques Jhoni on Tremé for 26 episodes. His other television credits include Heroes, The Riches, The Knick, and The Lincoln Lawyer. On the big screen, you may have seen him in Blood Diamond, Queen of Katwe, or Farewell Amor.

Claudette is an NYPD detective who worked with Harrison in season 1 and could pose a threat to Dexter for obvious reasons.

Kadia Saraf played a U.S. Attorney on seven episodes of Law & Order: Special Victims Unit and reprised her role on Law & Order: Organized Crime. She popped up on many other procedural shows before and has continued to since, including FBI, Blue Bloods, Rescue Me, and The Good Fight.

Fiona is an NYPD training officer who befriends Harrison. Her father, played by Bokeem Woodbine, is the captain.

Prior to Dexter: Resurrection, Nona Parker Johnson also played a police detective on Law & Order: Organized Crime. Her other television credits include recurring roles on Fear the Walking Dead, Mayor of Kingstown, and Sugar.

Krysten Ritter plays Mia LaPierre aka Lady Vengeance

Mia is a member of a former social club for serial killers who met and befriended Dexter in the first season. Dexter has not decided to “do what he does” with her yet.

Krysten Ritter is doing double duty on the fictional streets of New York City these days. She also plays the street-level superhero Jessica Jones on Daredevil: Born Again, reprising her role from Netflix’s various Marvel series in the Defenders universe. She’s also known for Don’t Trust the B in Apt 23, Breaking Bad, Veronica Mars, Gilmore Girls, Orphan Black: Echoes, 27 Dresses, Mona Lisa Smile, and Vamps.

Brian Cox plays Don Framt a.k.a. the New York Ripper

Don is a former serial killer taunting his “alleged” victims.

Hopefully you know Brian Cox from Succession and not just the McDonald’s commercials... but they are very good commercials! He is an Olivier, Emmy, BAFTA, and Golden Globe-winning actor. His film credits include The Fantastic Mr. Fox, Her, Zodiac, Rise of the Planet of the Apes, X2, Troy, Adaptation, and Braveheart. Prior to Dexter and Succession, his television credits included War & Peace, Penny Dreadful, The Straits, Kings, Deadwood, and an Emmy-nominated guest turn on Frasier.

Dan Stevens plays Lucas Stark a.k.a. the Five Borough Killer

rLucas is another new serial killer this season. He is actively killing and wants attention. Or he’s willing to risk attention to prove that the NYPD can’t catch him even with ample warning.

Dan Stevens was best known for playing Matthew Crawley on Downton Abbey. His career has kind of taken only left turns since. (Metaphorically. I know geographically that’s a circle.) He played the Beast in Disney’s live-action Beauty and the Beast. He starred as the Marvel Comics antihero David Haller on the FX series Legion. He had a leading role in the recent season of The Terror, and his other film credits include The Guest, Abigail, Eurovision, Cuckoo, and the recent Godzilla x Kong movies.

Gabriel Luna plays Ray Ballard a.k.a. the Sleepy-Eyed Stranger

Finally, Ray is a third new serial killer this season. Will he be bested by Dexter, or be the one to best him? We’ll have to wait and see.

Luna is best known these days for playing Tommy on HBO’s The Last of Us. He was also a version of the Marvel comics character Ghost Rider on Agents of S.H.I.E.L.D. and played a Terminator called Rev-9 in Terminator: Dark Fate. You might also know him from shows like Matador, The Terminal List, True Detective, and Wicked City.

#6945. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

#6944. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.
