#6940. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

These sumptuous knits are anything but frumpy.

Ahead, see the evening sweater trends to shop for the colder days ahead.

#6936. 5 Easy Ways to Meet People in a New City, According to Experts

Yes, you do have to put yourself out there. But I promise these tips make that just a little less daunting to do.

Whether it’s for a new job, your partner, or just needing a change, moving to a new city is a step into the unknown. I’ve done that very thing several times throughout my life, and one thing is certain: meeting people in a new city requires effort.

Last year, after my dog, Bowie, passed away just two weeks after I got laid off from my job, I took it as a sign from the universe to temporarily relocate to Naples, Italy. Not only was this a step away from everything I knew, but it also meant trying to make new friends in both a city and country that were not my own. The verdict? Some days were a struggle, but overall, I can now say I have friends in Naples.

So all that’s to say: it’s possible, dear reader. And just in case you don’t want to solely take my word for it, I went ahead and tapped a few experts on the matter. Below, they share a few tips on just how to meet people—both romantic and platonic—in a new city.

Not only have I moved to new cities throughout my life, but I’m also a frequent solo traveler. One of my favorite ways to socialize is by joining food tours or group day trips. I have made lifelong friends from simple walking tours, so it’s no surprise the experts suggest this.

Group activities can vary, of course—from dancing classes to team sports—but it ultimately comes down to just “doing something together,” says sexologist Melanie Eichhorn, who’s head of sexual education and diversity at Satisfyer. “It gives you a natural reason to talk, and you see the same faces week after week.” This can be a group workout class, an activity you already know you enjoy, or a new hobby.

“Hobbies are a great way to meet new people, since you'll often have something immediately in common,” adds Eloise Skinner, an author and psychotherapist. “You’ll also have an activity to complete, which can help reduce any anxiety about not knowing what to do or say.” Skinner suggests following your interests. If you love crafting, find a crafting club. Or if reading is your thing, search up a few book clubs or pop into a indie bookstore to see if they’re offering one.

Group activities are a great way to meet friends, yes, but they also can be avenues that lead to a potential romantic partnership, whether with that person in the group or someone they may know. “Hobby-focused activities allow for a sense of consistency where you can get to know someone on a friendship level first, and then allow time for it to deepen into something else,” says Skinner.

I did this by happenstance during my early days in Naples. I was walking around one day and struggling with my phone, so I popped into a small bar to use their WiFi. I got a glass of wine, connected to their network, and ended up making small talk with the bartender. I started going back once or twice a week because it was a familiar place, and the bartender ended up introducing me to other regulars and his coworkers, and each of them became my friend. I’m living proof that this works, and the experts agree on the method.

“If you're often hanging out at particular places, you might have something in common with others who are in the same spaces,” Skinner explains. “For example, your local coffee shop, co-working space, or gym, these are sometimes known as 'third spaces,’ which can provide an opportunity for us to explore different parts of our identity and feel a sense of belonging in a deeper way than we might at work or at home.”

The idea is that, if you and someone frequent the same place, you already have something common, and there might be more shared interests between you.

Let’s say you’ve made at least one or two friends in your new city, but you’re looking for a romantic partnership or even more friends. There’s no harm in asking your new acquaintances to introduce you to people they know.

“Meeting friends through existing friends is a great way to connect,” Skinner says. “We already know that the new friend is likely to have some degree of overlap in values and lifestyle with our existing friend group, and it can be a safe way to connect with a stranger.”

Ask your old friends back in the city you moved away from too. “Maybe a friend from home knows someone in your new city and can connect you,” says Eichhorn. “Meeting through a mutual contact feels more relaxed, because you already have something in common.”

As someone who has moved many times, I hate to tell you that, unfortunately, you actually have to put yourself out there. For a social introvert, this might seem daunting, but it doesn’t have to be—events don’t always mean massive gatherings, and can sometimes be much smaller scale.

“Neighborhood events like street festivals, flea markets, or community gatherings are a great way to meet people who live nearby, and they make your new city feel like home much faster,” Eichhorn says. “Go where life is happening, and be willing to step outside your comfort zone. Say yes to invitations, start the conversation, suggest a coffee after class. It may feel awkward at first, but that small step is often what turns a familiar face into a real connection.”

Finally, don’t knock online community. This doesn’t just mean dating apps, though that is a viable choice, too. For example, you can search for volunteer work or things to do in local Facebook groups. Utilizing sites like Meetup.com or Eventbrite also provides opportunities to make friends without dating pressure.

“There are apps for romantic connections and apps for platonic friendships,” Eichhorn says. “They let you find out in a low-pressure way whether someone is interested in you before you meet in person. For many shy people, that first step is much easier to take from behind a screen. It can also help to set yourself small, realistic challenges.”

When putting yourself out there, whether for new friends, a new job, or a new partner, rejection is going to be part of the process. Eichhorn suggests it’s good to hear it. “Remember: the worst thing that can happen is a ‘no,’” she says. “You can even see that as a chance to practice handling it calmly, which is a valuable skill in every part of life.”

#6935. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

#6931. How to Spot an Incel in Plain Sight, According to Experts

I’ll never forget my first incel encounter. It was just last year in 2025. I had wrote a story entitled I thought by 34, I’d be married with kids. Instead, I just took a solo trip to my 90th country. I received a DM from a lovely follower saying, “This man made a hate video about you.” I clicked on the link and there it was: a man with a small following on YouTube had made a 25-minute video depicting my lifestyle, my body, my choices, which was entirely triggered by my published piece.

Sometimes though, incels aren’t this upfront. So how exactly do you know when you’re interacting with one? Are the signs always obvious and noticeable right away, or are there more nuanced and subtle indicators to look out for? Below, two experts tell us all the signs to look out for when trying to spot an incel.

It’s short for “involuntarily celibate” and in simple terms it’s a resentful person (overwhelmingly male, though not exclusive) that can’t get laid and takes it out on society, namely women. Incels are part of an online subculture where discussing ideologies rooted in misogyny are encouraged, often leading to extremist behavior.

“It's important to understand that incel ideology doesn't just appear out of nowhere,” Rachel Overvoll, somatic sexologist and AASECT-certified sex educator. “It's part of a much larger online ecosystem—the manosphere—where misogyny is often packaged as dating advice, self-improvement, or men reclaiming their masculinity.”

This shouldn’t be confused with the male loneliness epidemic; it’s important to keep the distinction separate. “Being lonely, struggling with dating, or not having sex doesn't automatically make someone an incel,” says pleasure educator Carly S. “Plenty of people experience those things without developing a deep resentment toward women.”

When it comes to identifying an incel, there are some clear indicators: “One of the biggest warning signs that someone might be involved in incel culture is how they talk about women,” says Carly S., who is also the owner of Horny Stoner.

Overvoll agrees with Carly S., adding that it’s also necessary to “pay attention to how someone talks about rejection and boundaries,” she says. “Do they interpret rejection as an injustice rather than another person's right to make a choice? Do they use words or phrases like ‘high value,’ ‘chad,’ ‘golddigger,’ or ‘sexual gatekeeper?’ These are all phrases widely used in incel spaces.”

How a man talks about violence against women is imperative info too. One thing you can evaluate, says Overvoll, is “do they believe women when women say they were harmed by a man, or do they make a case that it was the woman’s fault?”

Sometimes, their algorithm will give it away. “It can start with content about confidence or relationships and gradually normalize the idea that women are the problem, that men are entitled to certain things from women, and that having power over a partner is somehow a sign of strength,” explains Overvoll. “That's what makes the red pill pipeline so concerning.”

If you’ve seen Don’t Worry, Darling, then you know incels can literally go to extreme lengths. But while not every incel kidnaps you and straps you into an alternate-reality simulation like Harry Styles’s character did in the movie, there are other genuine concerns about them.

“Entitlement breeds contempt,” Overvoll says. “When someone stops seeing their partner as an equal human being with their own needs and agency, the risk of [intimate partner violence] increases.”

“Gaslighting is another concern,” Carly S. adds. “You could find yourself constantly having your feelings dismissed, being told you're overreacting, or being made to feel guilty for having perfectly reasonable expectations. Over time, that kind of behavior can really mess with your confidence and make you start questioning yourself.”

#6928. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6927. How to Wear Velvet Pants, According to the Runways

From Burberry’s utilitarian twist to Tory Burch’s stripes, the runways make the case for wearing velvet pants every day.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When you think of velvet, what comes to mind? For me, it has always been a cozy-chic holiday fabric. But that’s no longer the whole story. Velvet has graduated from special-occasion dressing to an everyday staple.

Designers like Tory Burch, Stella McCartney, and Burberry have shown that velvet can live alongside the rest of your wardrobe, not just next to your holiday party picks. The texture is cozy yet elevated, which is why it’s gaining more creative freedom with every season.

The easiest way to start is to treat velvet pants like any other trouser you’d reach for on repeat. Pair them with a fine-gauge knit, a crisp white shirt, or a matching tailored blazer, and let the fabric’s soft sheen do the talking. Swap in loafers or flats for the day, then switch to heels and a silk blouse for evening.

With velvet’s texture already doing the heavy lifting, accessories can stay simple and fuss-free. Wear them a few times, and you’ll realize just how versatile velvet trousers can be.

Below, get the runway-inspired edition of how to wear velvet pants this fall.

Velvet in bold jewel tones, as shown at Akris, makes more than a textural statement. For the full velvet effect, pair your trousers with a matching blazer and a coat in an equally soft, plush texture.

At Polo Ralph Lauren, wide-leg velvet trousers were styled with a knit layered over a collared button-up, a preppy look with a plush twist.

Consider the Ralph Lauren Collection approach: a romantic sheer ruffled blouse paired with a velvet duster coat.

Proenza Schouler’s tailored velvet suit pairs sharp attitude with a hint of playfulness.

Tory Burch proved that velvet and stripes are a perfect match. Pair velvet trousers with a simple henley tee, then add a statement accessory to finish it. A sculptural belt, dangly earrings, and oversized sunglasses give the outfit a focal point without competing with the velvet’s texture.

#6923. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6918. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

#6916. The Hirshhorn’s New Sculpture Garden Is a Vision of America

The Smithsonian institution’s newly transformed outdoor gallery opens as a wider debate over art, history, and the national identity has spilled onto the National Mall in Washington, D.C.

In Washington, D.C., in early September, in West Potomac Park just off the National Mall, workers in a fenced-off section of an athletic field were seen smoothing freshly poured concrete. They were preparing, it turned out, for the installation of two aywans—traditional Uzbek open-sided pavilions, with elaborately carved wooden columns and roofs. The pavilions were a gift from Uzbekistan’s president, Shavkat Mirziyoyev, to President Trump and the American people, presented as a symbol of “friendship and strategic partnership” between the countries amid ongoing trade negotiations and to mark America’s 250th anniversary this year.

The aywans were being installed at the site Trump has designated for his proposed National Garden of American Heroes, a park featuring 250 life-size statues of notable Americans. (In addition to past presidents and political figures, Mark Twain, Harriet Tubman, Martin Luther King Jr., Julia Child, Kobe Bryant, and curiously, the late Canadian-born longtime host of Jeopardy!, Alex Trebek, were among the early names floated.) The federal call for artists specified that the statues in the garden would be realistic and classical, and be made from traditional materials like marble, granite, bronze, copper, or brass. The development is also envisioned to include reflecting pools, plazas, cafés, and an amphitheater.

The National Garden of Heroes remains, officially, a preliminary concept still subject to federal review, with questions over congressional authorization looming—although Uzbekistan’s ambassador has said that the aywans will become part of it.

But in Washington, D.C., a city built on symbolism, the distinction between what is planned and what is already taking shape can be a slippery one. The capital—and the Mall in particular—has always been a place where ideas about what America is and represents are writ especially large. The monuments, memorials, protests, performances, celebrations, and public art have all helped define what we choose to commemorate, project, and remember. As the Trump administration undertakes its own recasting of those civic spaces—from the “American Flag Blue” resurfacing of the Reflecting Pool to the proposed 250-foot arch—the landscape is one where competing visions of America are continuing to be made manifest, often in real time.

A little over a mile northeast of where the aywans were being installed, off the Mall itself, another kind of garden was finding its form. At the Hirshhorn, the Smithsonian’s national museum of modern and contemporary art, the finishing touches were being put on a four-year transformation of the museum’s outdoor sculpture garden, which has been redesigned by the Japanese artist and architect Hiroshi Sugimoto.

The project was the culmination of now former Hirshhorn director Melissa Chiu’s 12-year tenure leading the museum; at the end of August, she left to become director of the Guggenheim in New York. When the garden opens on October 31, it will bring together nearly 60 works spanning more than a century of sculpture, from pieces in founder Joseph H. Hirshhorn’s collection to recent acquisitions and commissions by a diverse range of artists working today. But the garden is also in many ways a distillation of some of the more essential questions that Chiu sought to address during her time at the museum—chiefly around who contemporary art is for, what gets represented in the collection, and how to think about the remit of a national museum in a country as vast and diverse as the United States.

In 1939, Congress called for a museum of contemporary art in Washington, D.C., to complement the more classical National Gallery of Art. The idea was eventually realized in the 1960s, when Smithsonian Secretary S. Dillon Ripley persuaded financier and philanthropist Joseph H. Hirshhorn, a Latvian-born Jewish immigrant who had amassed a significant collection of modern art, to donate his collection. Hirshhorn was particularly interested in 19th- and early 20th-century sculpture, owning major works by Auguste Rodin, Pablo Picasso, Henri Matisse, Alberto Giacometti, Alexander Calder, and Henry Moore, while also collecting the work of then-living American artists like Willem de Kooning, Milton Avery, Stuart Davis, Arshile Gorky, Edward Hopper, and Larry Rivers. An Act of Congress established the Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden in 1966, and it opened on the Mall in 1974.

Designed by Gordon Bunshaft, the museum was conceived as a piece of sculpture itself—a monumental, elevated concrete cylinder with a sunken garden that broke sharply with the neoclassical architecture of the other institutions around it. Its holdings have since grown to more than 13,000 works, but its mandate remained distinctly forward-looking: to serve as a national museum for the art and artists of its own time.

I spoke with Chiu in late August, as she wound down her time at the Hirshhorn. Washington, D.C., was in the throes of preparing for the Freedom 250 Grand Prix, the IndyCar street race to mark the country’s 250th-anniversary celebrations. (The White House, which worked to clear the way for the race via executive order, described it as an event designed to “showcase the majesty” of Washington and its national monuments.) In the days leading up to the race, the area around the Mall was being transformed into a temporary racetrack: streets were closed, barricades were erected, and increased security disrupted traffic flow through the city. At one point during our conversation, Chiu had to step away to take a call because someone she was expecting was having trouble getting to the museum amid all the obstructions.

The brief interruption offered another D.C.-sized metaphor. The Australia-born Chiu, who spent 13 years at Asia Society in New York before coming to the Hirshhorn in 2014, arrived thinking about what it meant for the institution to be not simply another museum of modern and contemporary art, but a national museum dedicated to that work.

The museum came to refer to its approach as “radical accessibility”; Chiu said the idea was ultimately about more than making contemporary art easier to understand. “We described it much later and articulated it as being artist-centric,” she said. If a visitor was confronted with a seemingly difficult or complex work, the question was how best to help that person appreciate it. “It was really actually the artist,” she said, rather than “an abstracted art-historical explanation.”

That meant reducing the physical and intellectual barriers that might stand between visitors and the artist whose work they were seeing and rethinking everything from the tone of the wall texts and educational materials to the museum’s voice, as well as developing Hirshhorn Eye, a mobile guide that allows visitors to listen to artists talk about their own work. It also meant finding ways to extend the museum’s reach beyond its own walls, through digital projects, video, and even television. (The Exhibit: Finding the Next Great Artist, a six-part competition series, aired on MTV and the Smithsonian Channel in 2023).

The Hirshhorn’s audience is unusually broad for a museum of contemporary art. Because it is free and sits on the Mall, many visitors arrive with little expectation that they are viewing one of the country’s most important collections of modern and contemporary art. Some may be encountering that kind of work for the first time.

“I do think there is a kind of responsibility that we hold to our public given that we are that kind of public institution on the National Mall,” Chiu said. “It’s a different way of being in the world, I think,” she explains. “We tried to be less institutional, less formal.”

The Hirshhorn’s original collection, Chiu said, represented “the absolute canon of sculpture in the 20th century.” But canons today can represent a lot of different things to different people. Chiu’s goal was to ensure that a museum founded to reflect the present truly reflected the way artists now work across disciplines, using new technologies and materials, drawing on different traditions and histories, popular culture, and their own individual backgrounds and experiences. During her tenure, the Hirshhorn mounted major surveys of artists such as Shirin Neshat, Robert Irwin, Yayoi Kusama, Laurie Anderson, and Charline von Heyl. Kusama’s “Infinity Mirrors” show, in particular, became a phenomenon, helping to propel the museum’s annual attendance past one million in 2017.

The new sculpture garden is perhaps the clearest expression of that project. Major works from the original collection will return, including Rodin’s The Burghers of Calais and Monument to Balzac, Matisse’s complete series of The Backs I–IV, and works by Moore, Giacometti, Barbara Hepworth, David Smith, and Lucio Fontana. They will be joined by recent acquisitions and commissions that substantially widen the geographical and cultural frame by artists like Lauren Halsey, Raven Halfmoon, Mark Grotjahn, Liz Larner, Woody De Othello, Izumi Kato, Pedro Reyes, and Chatchai Puipia. Among them: Halfmoon’s Dancing at Dusk, which draws on Caddo traditions and contemporary Native life; Halsey’s keepers of the krown (antoinette grace halsey), a monumental column topped with a portrait of her grandmother; Kato’s Untitled, which transforms stones sourced in Japan into an otherworldly aluminum figure; Puipia’s Wish You Were Here, a reclining bronze wrapped in a traditional Thai pha khao ma cloth; and Pedro Reyes’s Tonatiuh, carved from volcanic stone and connected to pre-Columbian and Modernist Mexican traditions.

The garden will also feature KAWS’s SHELTER, a 14-foot bronze depicting two of his familiar COMPANION figures as parent and child; Yoko Ono’s Wish Tree for Washington, D.C., which invites visitors to leave behind handwritten wishes for peace; and Elmgreen & Dragset’s Eight, a 28-foot-tall vertical swimming pool complete with diving board, ladder, and lights. On the West Lawn, Yoshitomo Nara will inaugurate a new series of commissions with three monumental white heads—Long Tall Peace Sister, Smiling, and Tiny Bright Round Eyes—enlargements of small clay heads he made in 2021. In the aftermath of the 2011 Tōhoku earthquake and Fukushima nuclear disaster, Nara found himself unable to paint and turned to clay.

The original garden, designed by Bunshaft, was spare, recessed, and largely devoid of shade or greenery. Bunshaft had emulated the Zen gardens he visited in Japan, creating an austere setting in which the sculptures could stand apart from their surroundings. But it wasn’t exactly welcoming. Visitors entering from the Mall had to descend a flight of stairs, only to encounter a 10-foot concrete wall. Within a decade, landscape architect Lester Collins was brought in to soften the space, adding canopy trees, more vegetation, additional seating, and a ramp. But by the 2010s the garden was also contending with deteriorating infrastructure and leaks in the passageway that connected it to the museum plaza. More fundamentally, it had become increasingly ill-equipped for the scale and variety of work the museum was presenting.

To remake it, Chiu turned to Sugimoto, who already had a long relationship with the Hirshhorn, which presented a survey of his work in 2006. They had already collaborated on a redesign of the museum’s lobby, unveiled in 2018, which sought to work with Bunshaft’s architecture rather than overwrite it.

Sugimoto approached the garden as a place for people to engage with art rather than simply an architectural intervention. He widened its entrance and brought it up to the same grade as the Mall. He created long sightlines and a sequence of open and intimate spaces defined by a series of dry-stacked stone walls constructed from roughly 950 tons of stone. He also reopened the passageway connecting the garden to the museum plaza, transforming it into a sculptural tunnel.

Sugimoto said his goal was to work in dialogue with Bunshaft’s original design rather than erase it. “Architecture must reflect the spirit of the age in which it was built,” he explained. “That is what makes a great work of architecture endure in history.” His aim was to revive and expand some of the original ideas behind Bunshaft’s garden by looking backward as well as forward. “In considering what backdrop would best suit the viewing of modern sculpture,” he said, “I concluded that it should be a pre-modern one.”

He also reinterpreted the Japanese karesansui, or dry landscape garden, where minimalist arrangements of rocks and raked gravel evoke mountains and water. “It is expressed as a void within space, suggesting presence through absence,” he explained. “Rather than flaunting monumentality through size or height, I intended a serene space that sits quietly buried within its environment.”

The works on the West Lawn will change annually, while performances, educational programs, and other activities keep the space in motion. “It really is an invitation,” Chiu said of the redesign. “It’s an invitation to come inside.” The wider entrance from the Mall and the at-grade approach are a big part of that, allowing visitors to enter more easily and establishing a different relationship between the art and the audience. “Lauren Halsey’s grandmother will be at eye level with them now.”

The garden is a distillation of what Chiu said she tried to do throughout her time at the Hirshhorn: preserve the museum’s inheritance while expanding the definition of who, what, and whose histories belong in the collection—and, more broadly, what it means to be a national museum of contemporary art in a country like the United States

That question has taken on a new kind of urgency amid the Trump administration’s ongoing attempt to influence the way the Smithsonian institutions present American history and culture. Over the last year and a half, the administration has repeatedly threatened to withhold federal funding and other forms of government support over what it considers ideologically slanted material. This past summer, the White House released a 162-page report accusing the National Museum of American History of “extreme political activism” and objecting to what it characterized as a presentation of the country through “regret, tragedy, and shame.” Officials were directed to install temporary signs outside the museum warning visitors about what the administration deemed “inaccurate information” about American history.

In September, Trump called for the Smithsonian to install an 11-foot statue of George Washington in the National Museum of American History’s Flag Hall and, more strikingly, to replace Infinity, a 1967 abstract sculpture by José de Rivera outside the museum, with a 30-foot statue of Washington. He also called for a five-year exhibition devoted to Washington leading up to the 300th anniversary of his birth in 2032. “Replace a sculpture that says nothing about America with the man who, more than anyone else, says everything about America,” Trump posted on Truth Social (on September 11). Infinity is often said to be the first contemporary sculpture permanently installed on the Mall and, at the time, was the largest piece of contemporary sculpture ever commissioned by the federal government.

Earlier that month, Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie G. Bunch III announced he would retire at the end of the year after nearly four decades at the institution and seven years as secretary. Bunch said his decision was not related to the pressure from the administration, but his departure comes as the Smithsonian is navigating an increasingly public debate over its independence and authority.

Chiu, who I spoke with before Bunch’s announcement, was careful not to characterize the moment simply as an attack on museums, but part of a larger shift in the relationship between museums and the public. “I would say that over my tenure, I’ve seen different groups provide feedback on what they think museums should be doing and what they think museums should be showing, and that feedback is much more visible today, because a visitor can leave a message in the comments or post on social media,” she said. “Before, there was no feedback loop, no feedback mechanism, so I’d say that that has been going on for close to the last decade, actually—and yes, the past 18 months, it’s been a different kind of feedback loop that is very much about how Smithsonian museums present American history.”

She did not see that heightened scrutiny simply as a threat. “The fact that people care enough to voice concern and commitment about what their museums are doing, how they’re presenting the artifacts or artworks, shows that it matters,” she said.

I asked Chiu if the responsibility she referred to earlier was different for a national museum in a country as vast and diverse as the U.S. than for a private one. “I think that if you’re a public museum, you receive funding from public entities and from the government, and so with that, there are certain responsibilities,” she said. “One of them is that we’re free. Another is that we’re open every day but one throughout the entire year. And then the other, at least in the way that we think about it at the Hirshhorn, is that we are representative of different views by artists.”

In Washington, D.C., public sculpture has traditionally been used to declare, to commemorate, to fix history in place. The Hirshhorn’s new garden, Chiu suggested, reflects a country whose cultural history has never belonged to a single tradition. “I think perhaps here in America, more than anywhere else, this has been a country of many stories,” she told me. “I see what we’ve done in the sculpture garden as being representative of this country, that it was always one that welcomed artists from everywhere.”

As the Hirshhorn’s new sculpture garden prepares to open this weekend just days ahead of the midterm elections on November 3, the landscape around it continues to evolve. Earlier this month, the National Park Service unveiled a revised plan for the Tidal Basin and West Potomac Park that includes a new Great Lawn and Oval Park near the site that has been proposed for the National Garden of American Heroes. The garden itself is not part of the Park Service’s current plan, officials said, but preservationists immediately questioned whether the new configuration could ultimately make room for it.

I asked Chiu what she hoped to find when she returned to the Hirshhorn. “The best possible thing as a museum director that you can see is people,” she said. “Just people enjoying art.”

#6914. Ben Ahlers Teases Jack and Bridget’s “Tangible, Objective Obstacle” in The Gilded Age Season 4

Does entering his gentleman era mean leaving her behind?

The rise of Jack Trotter—or as the internet calls him, “Clock Twink”—has been one of The Gilded Age’s most rewarding storylines. He started as a humble footman in the Van Rhijn household and got rich practically overnight after inventing an affordable alarm clock—all while somehow avoiding becoming a terrible person. He is one of the easiest characters on the show to root for. Jack could do practically anything onscreen and I would be thrilled.

His will-they-won’t-they relationship with Bridget, one of the Van Rhijn household’s maids, is part of that. Fans have been hoping for these two to get together for multiple seasons now. But after a close call last season, they seem further apart than ever. The actor behind Jack, Ben Ahlers, chatted with Cosmopolitan about what season 4 has in store for this maybe-couple and whether Jack will be able to stay true to himself as he climbs the social ladder.

What's so interesting about this whole world is the public and private self, and Jack has become a master of that. He has a private self downstairs in the Van Rhijn household as the trickster, the one who can poke at Armstrong or keep the vibe going when everybody's having a long day. My accent would change between the two, upstairs and downstairs, over the last few seasons. But now he's in a completely different scenario where he doesn't have that family. He doesn't have the private self. As he's stepping into these new worlds with inherently manipulative, distrustful, power-hungry people, there's a level of caution.

You don't become a gentleman overnight. You don't make 300 grand and just become a swaggy dude. There's an acclimating that has to happen, and I really enjoyed playing with that tension. A lot of what you see of him over this season is him trying to find the people and the places where he can reconnect with that beautiful, loving private self.

I think he has an opportunity to more so than anybody because he is carving his own path. He was an orphan who grew up in servitude and then elevated into the upper class. There's no one else who has that track. He has the money and the skill set to provide a life for himself. But he doesn't have the support of Banister patting him on the back to tell him to keep going. It’s going to be a challenge. This isolation and new beginning is a really incredible curriculum for the type of man that I know he can be.

Jack and Bridget have one of the most foundational relationships in his life. It's been a bit nebulous. They were kind of romantic, and then they were friends, and then there are glimmers of maybe something happening at the end of season 3. It's a really unique but relatable challenge that when you're in an interpersonal relationship with that much love, how do you foster change in one another, necessary growth when that doesn't always align? Now they're in two separate classes, and that's going to create a tangible, objective obstacle. And I'm really hopeful for them to find that sense of intimacy and connection, regardless of the sort of stations of their lives.

It's so fun to play in that tension, especially with a great scene partner, because you get to try out different tactics and you get to try it at a different event or a party, and sometimes you connect and then it breaks away again. That's what makes people gravitate towards stories like this, because that expansion contraction internally and interpersonally is the whole bag that all of us are going through every day of our lives.

#6913. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

In March 2024, Bündchen confirmed that she and Valente were together. In an interview with The New York Times, she said, “This is the first time I am seeing someone that was a friend of mine first. It’s very different. It is very honest, and it’s very transparent.”

#6909. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

#6908. The Top Five Brands to Know This Winter

With sharp tailoring and standout knitwear, MR PORTER offers a variety of collections for dressing well in the cold.

Winter cold gives you a reason to wear the clothes that make getting dressed interesting: a proper wool coat, a substantial sweater, trousers with some weight to them, and boots built for more than walking from the car to the restaurant. Then the holiday parties start, and your usual uniform needs an upgrade. A good wardrobe should handle all of it without requiring you to develop a different personality for every occasion. And because you’re wearing more of your clothes at once, the details matter. Fit, fabric, and proportion can make the difference between looking put together and looking like you got dressed in the dark.

MR PORTER is a great place to start building that versatile wardrobe. Its assortment brings together brands with a clear understanding of what each does well, from Tom Ford’s sharp tailoring to Brunello Cucinelli’s easy sophistication and Begg & Co’s expertise in knitwear and accessories. Yoke offers a more experimental take on everyday dressing, while Mr P focuses on the everyday staples that make getting dressed easier. The common threads are thoughtful design, good materials, and construction that holds up to repeated wear.

We’ve picked two pieces from each brand that make a compelling case for adding them to your cold-weather wardrobe.

Tom Ford launched his namesake label in 2005 after revitalizing Gucci throughout the 1990s with a sexier, more provocative take on luxury. The brand is known for sharp tailoring, sleek silhouettes, and a glamorous sensibility that extends from eveningwear to leather goods and footwear. Today, designer Haider Ackermann leads its creative direction, carrying that legacy forward with his own modern vision.

The designer’s tailoring has always favored a sharp silhouette, and these brown wool tuxedo pants put a more relaxed spin on formalwear. Double pleats give the straight legs room to move, while pressed creases keep the shape crisp. Wear them with a black dinner jacket for a holiday party that calls for something beyond the usual uniform, or pair them with a fine-gauge black sweater and polished loafers for a winter dinner.

A good winter night-out look starts from the ground up. Tom Ford’s Chelsea boots bring a little rock-and-roll swagger to the occasion, with glossy, lizard-effect leather, sharply pointed toes, and stacked heels that give the classic slip-on silhouette a Western edge. They’re the sort of boots that can turn a dark suit into a statement or give black jeans and a wool coat some much-needed attitude. Wear them to a holiday party, a late dinner, or anywhere your sneakers might send the wrong message.

Founded in Umbria in 1978, Brunello Cucinelli began with a simple idea: make cashmere sweaters for women in colors that felt fresh and modern. The Italian label has since expanded into a full menswear and womenswear collection, becoming known for luxurious knitwear, fine tailoring, and an easy approach to dressing well. Its clothes combine traditional Italian craftsmanship with a relaxed sophistication that works just as well for everyday wear as it does for more formal occasions.

Brunello Cucinelli has built a following around luxurious fabrics and Italian tailoring that manage to feel relaxed. This wool-felt overshirt puts that approach to work in a more casual silhouette, with flap chest pockets, silver-tone snap fastenings, and a curved hem. The black wool is substantial enough to take the edge off a cold city morning, while the clean design makes it a smart alternative to a traditional jacket. Wear it over a fine-knit sweater with wool trousers for the office, then head straight to dinner.

Brunello Cucinelli’s cashmere expertise is on full display in this black ribbed zip-up sweater, knitted in Italy with a relaxed fit and a stand collar that keeps the cold off your neck. The two-way zipper lets you adjust the coverage, whether you’re layering it under a wool overcoat for a weekend spent exploring the city or wearing it on its own during a long flight. It’s a clever way to make travel-day comfort feel considerably more considered.

Mr P. has an advantage most menswear labels don’t: it was developed by the people at MR PORTER who spend their days studying what makes clothes worth buying. Launched in 2017, the in-house brand focuses on wardrobe staples, from knitwear and denim to outerwear, with an emphasis on quality materials and details that make sense in everyday life. The idea is to get the fundamentals right, then give men plenty of ways to wear them.

A polo shirt can do more than a button-down when the occasion calls for looking presentable without getting too dressed up. Mr P.’s long-sleeved version is knitted in Italy from 75 percent merino wool and 25 percent silk, giving the fine-gauge fabric a soft feel and subtle sheen. An open collar and ribbed trims keep the design streamlined. A smart alternative to another crewneck sweater, this can be worn with flannel trousers for a holiday lunch or under a tailored coat when you’re meeting friends for a winter matinee.

Some winter days require a coat that does more than keep you warm. Mr P.’s green shearling number has plenty of personality, with a suede exterior, plush lining, and a broad collar that frames the face. The loose fit leaves room for a chunky sweater underneath, while the button front and slanted pockets keep things practical. Wear it with dark denim and sturdy boots for a Saturday spent browsing record shops, grabbing lunch, or walking home the long way. You might even forget how cold it is.

Founded by designer Norio Terada in Tokyo in 2018, Yoke takes its name from the garment panel that joins pieces of fabric together. That idea of connection carries through the label’s approach to design, which draws on art and reworks familiar menswear with unexpected proportions, interesting textures, and subtle twists. The result is clothing that feels relaxed and easy to wear, with enough edge to keep a wardrobe of everyday staples from looking predictable.

Yoke approaches everyday clothing with a Japanese design sensibility that puts proportion and considered details at the forefront. These wide-leg jeans show how much a change in silhouette can do. Cut from black cotton denim, they have a roomy leg and a classic five-pocket design, with enough volume to balance a chunky knit or a boxy winter coat. Wear them with a pair of leather sneakers for an afternoon antique hunting or checking out a gallery.

Made in Japan from a blend of 85 percent wool and 15 percent cashmere, this gray felt overcoat has a modern silhouette withdropped shoulders, wide notch lapels, and an oversized double-breasted cut that leaves plenty of room for winter layers. The fabric feels substantial without weighing you down, making it the perfect choice for days when you’re walking across town rather than hopping between cabs. Throw it over a chunky sweater and wide-leg jeans for a look that feels polished without being overly formal.

Begg & Co’s story begins in Paisley, Scotland, in 1866, when Alex Begg established a textile business that would become known for its scarves and cashmere. Today, the brand makes knitwear and accessories at mills in Ayr and Hawick, combining traditional Scottish textile techniques with modern design. Its cashmere collection ranges from classic scarves to substantial sweaters, with texture and color often doing the work that logos do elsewhere.

Begg & Co has been making textiles in Scotland since the 19th century, and its experience shows in the details of this navy scarf. The Bernard is knitted from a blend of 56 percent cashmere and 44 percent lambswool, with a repeating cable pattern that gives it texture and wide ribbed ends that keep the design clean. At nearly 7 feet long, it can be wrapped snugly around your neck on a windy morning or left loose over a wool coat when you want a little more warmth on the way to a weekend lunch.

Begg & Co’s Surfer cardigan is knitted in Scotland from pure cashmere, with blended yarns that give the ribbed surface subtle tonal variation. Horn buttons, welt pockets, and a shawl collar lend it a classic look, while the substantial knit makes it a practical alternative to a lightweight jacket. Wear it over a T-shirt with relaxed trousers for a slow Sunday morning at home, then keep it on when you head out for coffee or an impromptu lunch. It’s an easy way to look put together without getting dressed up.

#6904. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#6903. Mckenna Grace Is a Bigger ‘Hunger Games’ Fan Than You

The actor has a way of approaching everything dialed up to 100: her work, her relationships, her fangirl enthusiasm. With Sunrise on the Reaping, it really paid off.

Mckenna Grace is operating on two hours of sleep when she opens her Zoom window. Fresh off a night shoot, she is losing her voice and has the beginnings of a black eye from taking an accidental knee to the face. She uses the camera as a mirror to check for damage, joking, “I’m so thankful that this is what I get to do and have my butt kicked on.”

She goes on to enthusiastically detail how her days on set have been filled with snake wranglers, poison ivy, and feral kittens found in the woods. It’s all very Hunger Games–coded and very fitting, of course, considering Mckenna stars in the upcoming Sunrise on the Reaping as Maysilee Donner, a snarky tribute from the fictional world of Panem’s District 12 (Mckenna also plays Maysilee’s twin sister, Merrilee, in the movie).

It’s an adventure and not one without risk, but she is very much here for it, having spent most of her life throwing herself—literally—into the universes she loves. From Scream to Ghostbusters to The Hunger Games, Mckenna has been working on her favorite franchises since she was 5 years old (“I grew up on sets, out of a suitcase,” she shares). Her IMDb page is already longer than a CVS receipt, with eight credits in 2025 alone, several in 2026, and the soon-to-premiere Netflix series Scooby-Doo: Origins, in which she plays Daphne. (If you’re curious about just how big a fan of that project Mckenna is, she named her dog, who she’s posing with on her Cosmopolitan cover, Scooby Doo and her cat Scrappy Mew.)

The enthusiasm bleeds into her relationships, too, and comes through in how she speaks about her boyfriend, her Regretting You costar Mason Thames (“my best friend”), and her Sunrise on the Reaping costar Joseph Zada, with whom she spent many nights playing chess while shooting in a remote village in Spain (“I beat him constantly. Put it in print”). Because one thing about the 20-year-old is crystal clear: Whatever she decides to tackle, she’ll be going all in.

The one thing it would require is Mckenna Grace. No, but I’m just out here clawing my way through, trying to get jobs and do things I’m proud of. It’s scary because unlike in the ’90s or early 2000s—where your favorite actors did the same thing in a bunch of movies—now with social media, people get really sick of seeing people really fast. It’s like, “This is the hot new commodity, the new face we’ve been wanting.” Three weeks later, everybody is hating on them.

The internet is a big part of the industry but very fickle. There was a time where people wouldn’t give you an interview because you had less than 3 million followers.1 Yet it’s a catch-22 because if you’re too present…but, hey, I’m just thankful to be here. And lately, I’ve been blessed to be able to do these franchises that I’m a huge fan of. I am the biggest Hunger Games fan.

1. Mckenna has a total of 13+ million social media followers.

I was very invested in all of it—the headcanons and the fan base. I had this huge Hunger Games poster. I got a bow and arrows so that I could be like Katniss. I told [director] Francis Lawrence when I got to set: “Dude, I’ve watched these movies and read these books a million times. I’ve edited these cornucopias. To be running off my own podium toward the cornucopia, this is not real to me.”

Why did it have to get back to Josh [Hutcherson] is my thing. I played it so cool for a year and a half of knowing him, and he never brought it up. But everyone keeps reminding him. Just let it die; let a girl have some dignity. I do regret it in a way, but I am also just like, “I am a real fan, a fangirl. I’m a CapCut warrior.”

I keep a journal for my characters with pages dedicated to fan things. For Maysilee, I’m like, Okay, they want to see the braid, so I’d pull Francis aside, like, “Look, man, she needs this braid in her hair; she needs this pin.” He’s like, “Sure, Mckenna.”

She does get a bad rap, and she has that snark to her, but she has a kind heart. She cares about the people she loves.

You think I’m going to slap Glenn Close? Hell no. I am terrified of her in the best way. I love her to death.

At this point, I definitely don’t do nudity or very hypersexual things. I respect anybody who can, but it’s terrifying to me. I don’t know if that’ll ever change. It’s not like I only do wholesome things—my character literally gets gutted by Ghostface in Scream—but my dream is to try and be a Disney princess someday.

As a kid on set, you’re expected to act like an adult until you’re on camera. Then you’re a kid until everybody is mad at you because you’re acting like a kid. Now it’s cool to be an adult and be able to give my opinion and have a bit more ownership over characters. I’ve really enjoyed sitting down with producers and pitching all my ideas.

I always wanted to go to a school dance. When prom season came around, I told all my friends who were single, “I don’t care where you are, I will come to your prom.” I ended up going to three proms. I didn’t know the people there. I just showed up, had a good time, and then dipped.

Your girl is happy. I’m feeling very lovey-dovey. I have somebody I can be a complete nerd with. I got my best friend, and so I don’t see the point in not loving loudly.2

2. Mckenna posted a birthday tribute in July to her boyfriend, Mason Thames, singing “honeybee” by Olivia Rodrigo. One commenter wrote, “This couple is the Gen Alpha Tom Holland and Zendaya.”

There are moments that are just for you and your person. But I don’t see the point in not celebrating who you’re in love with. A lot of people’s hesitation around this stems from the idea that if you post about someone a bunch and then you break up, that’s embarrassing. But why would I even think about that? I’m just going to focus on the present.

It’s a terrifying thing in a relationship to surrender yourself to that thought of, I’m just going to be at my most vulnerable and trust you, and this part of my life is fully in your hands. But I don’t see the point in building a relationship based on fear. I am the biggest fan of the person I’m with, so why wouldn’t I be like, “This is my person. I am so stoked I have this”? We’re always on each other’s sets anyway. He’s like an honorary member of District 12 at this point.

I hope to still be as excited waking up for work every day, even though it’s tiring and I get bruised and half the time people may hate me for things I can’t control. I’m thankful to still be feeling that way 15 years in, so I hope that 25 years in, I feel the exact same way.

3. For her birthday, Mckenna was supposed to go to the aquarium with her parents and Mason. Her shooting schedule ran too late, so her mom and Mason waited up to surprise her in shark onesies instead.

If I’m 80 and people are like, “Oh, you’re the girl from The Hunger Games,” I’ll be like, “Yes, I am. I did that. That’s so cool.”
